```yaml
feature_label: reported_prior_conversation
observation: "The dialogue opens in an outer frame: Crito asks Socrates about the previous day's conversation at the Lyceum, says he could not hear clearly because of the crowd, and asks who the interlocutor was."
textual_basis: "At 271a Crito marks the conversation as yesterday's Lyceum exchange, reports that a large crowd prevented clear hearing, and asks Socrates to identify the stranger. The frame is Crito's request for a report of an inaccessible prior conversation."
limits: "This records only the outer-frame setup in 271a. It does not include Socrates' later identification of both brothers or any interpretation of Crito's reliability."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 271a-271b, Crito first reports a stranger and an unnamed youth; Socrates then names the two brothers, and Crito says he knows neither."
```

```yaml
observation: "The conversation at the Lyceum attracted a large crowd that surrounded Socrates and the strangers, preventing Crito from hearing clearly and forcing him to rely on a visual glimpse by peering over to identify the interlocutors."
textual_basis: "At 271a, Crito reports that a large crowd was standing around Socrates and the strangers, such that although he wanted to listen he could hear nothing clearly. He could only peer over and see. This establishes the public, crowded setting as a dramatic circumstance that constrains the frame narrative."
limits: "This observation registers the dramatic setting of a public crowd but does not claim any argumentative function for the crowd or assess whether it recurs later in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_1309
feature_label: sophist_biography_in_narrative_frame
observation: Socrates supplies biographical details about Euthydemus and Dionysodorus in a narrative frame addressed to Crito: their genos is from Chios, they settled as colonists in Thurii, were exiled from there, and have now been wandering around the Athens region for many years.
textual_basis: At 271c Socrates identifies the pair as sophists and traces their movements from Chios to Thurii to exile and their current wandering near Athens. This is biographical data about named historical figures disclosed outside the dramatic dialogue itself, in the frame conversation with Crito.
limits: This record does not assess the historical accuracy of the biography. It does not claim that Socrates knows these facts firsthand. It does not address how the biography functions in the larger dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1310
feature_label: hyperbolic_praise_of_sophist
observation: Socrates describes the wisdom of Euthydemus and Dionysodorus with extreme praise, calling it "wonderful" and calling the brothers "all-wise, absolutely."
textual_basis: At 271c Socrates describes their wisdom with the adjective meaning "wonderful" and calls them a compound meaning "all-wise" intensified by an adverb meaning "absolutely." The intensifier and the superlative compound push the praise beyond ordinary commendation, creating a pattern of hyperbolic elevation consistent with Socratic irony.
limits: This record does not assert that Socrates is being insincere, only that the language is hyperbolic. It does not claim irony is the only possible reading. It does not assess the sophists' actual competence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 271c-271d Socrates says he did not previously know that pancratiasts existed, calls the brothers all-fighters, and contrasts them with Acarnanian pancratiast brothers who fought only with the body."

review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: refutation_indifferent_to_truth
observation: Socrates reports that Euthydemus and Dionysodorus have become skilled at refuting whatever is said, whether it is false or true.
textual_basis: At 272a-b, Socrates tells Crito that the two brothers have become so clever at fighting in speeches and refuting that no one could stand against them, and that they refute whatever is said alike whether it is false or true.
limits: This records Socrates' characterization of the brothers' eristic skill, not a demonstrated instance of the technique. The observation does not assess whether the brothers actually possess this ability or whether Socrates' report is reliable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: The observation records the presence of the combat-sport craft analogy. It does not determine whether the analogy originates with Socrates or with the brothers' self-presentation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1314
feature_label: named_craft_practitioner_with_patronymic
observation: Socrates names Connus, son of Metrobius, a lyre-teacher who still instructs him in lyre-playing, and reports that his young fellow-pupils laugh at him and mockingly call the teacher an "old-man-teacher."
textual_basis: At 272c, Socrates identifies Connus by personal name, patronymic (son of Metrobius), and craft designation as a kitharistes. The boys who study alongside Socrates call Connus by the comic compound gerontodidaskalos, meaning teacher of old men, and laugh at Socrates for being an elderly pupil.
limits: This passage provides prosopographical data about Connus as a character referenced in the text; it does not independently verify the historical Connus or his relationship to Socrates outside the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: embedded_narration_setup
observation: Crito requests that Socrates narrate the wisdom of the two strangers, and Socrates agrees to narrate everything from the beginning, explicitly framing the dialogue that follows as a remembered narration delivered to Crito.
textual_basis: At 272d, Crito asks Socrates to narrate (diegesai) what the wisdom of the two men consists of, so that Crito can know what they will learn. Socrates replies that he paid close attention and remembers, and will try to narrate everything from the beginning (ex arches hapanta diegesasthai).
limits: This confirms the narrative frame as an explicit structural device but does not by itself characterize the reliability, depth, or transmission mechanics of the embedded narration.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: [μακαρίζω, μέγαν βασιλέα]
english_gloss: "I count you happier in this possession than the Great King in his empire."
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1481
feature_label: socrates_makarismos_amplification
observation: "Socrates responds to the brothers' claim of being able to teach virtue by calling them 'blessed' (makarios) in their possession of wisdom, more so than the Great King in his empire. The comparison to the Great King's dominion magnifies the claim to absurd proportions."
textual_basis: "At 274a Socrates says he counts them blessed (makarios) in their possession far more than the Great King in his empire. The amplification from a wisdom-claim to surpassing the wealthiest monarch is characteristic of Socratic irony."
limits: "This observation does not determine whether Socrates is sincere or ironic; it notes only that the comparison to the Great King is a recognizable marker of exaggerated praise that, in context, functions as an irony signal."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: "This observation notes only that the daimonion is the dramatic cause of the encounter. It does not interpret what the daimonion is, whether it indicates divine approval or disapproval, or what Socrates' attitude toward it is beyond his compliance."
```

```yaml
observation: Euthydemus leads Cleinias through a short assent chain, using the example of the lyre-teacher, that reverses Cleinias's initial claim: instead of the wise learning, the ignorant learn.
textual_basis: At 276a to 276b, Euthydemus secures Cleinias's agreement that teachers teach learners (like the lyrist and writing master), that when the boys were learning they did not yet know the subject, that not knowing meant they were not wise but ignorant, and therefore the ignorant learn. This directly contradicts Cleinias's opening answer that the wise are the learners.
limits: This records the structure of the eristic refutation only; it does not assess whether the shift from the wise to those who know the subject is valid or whether the argument is sophistical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0037
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 277b-277c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 277b-277c
  start_marker: 277b
  end_marker: 277c
  start_char: 12450
  end_char: 13294
  text_sha256: 24fe08159fc176cc1a9c2316a3e895fb2c0a06407fefde083ec20fcbed3df28e
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1619
feature_label: equivocation_manthanein_epistasthai
observation: Dionysodorus traps Clinias by constructing an equivocation between two senses of manthanein (learning): acquiring new knowledge versus already having knowledge. He defines manthanein as "acquiring knowledge" (epistemen lambanein), then argues that those who already know (epistamenoi) are not acquiring, so only those who do not know (me epistamenoi) learn.
textual_basis: At 277b Dionysodorus asks whether manthanein is "acquiring knowledge of that which one learns" (epistemen esti lambanein toutou hou an tis manthanei). He then secures agreement that epistasthai is "already having knowledge" (echein epistemen ede), and that me epistasthai is "not yet having knowledge" (mepo echein epistemen). By conflating the state of knowing with the act of learning, he forces Clinias to concede that only the ignorant learn (277c: hoi me epistamenoi ara manthanousin, o Klineia, all' ouch hoi epistamenoi).
limits: This observation records the equivocation structure but does not evaluate whether the argument is sophistical or whether Plato endorses this distinction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Clinias assents at every step of Dionysodorus's chain: he agrees (homologei, synephe, homologei auto, kateneuse, synephe) five times in rapid succession across 277b-277c, with no objection or request for clarification.
textual_basis: The text marks five separate assent verbs in this short span: homologei at 277b, synephe at 277b, homologei auto at 277c, kateneuse (nodded) at 277c, and synephe at 277c. Each assent follows a short binary question, giving Clinias no room to interrogate the premises.
limits: This observation notes the density of assents and lack of resistance but does not claim that Clinias is coerced or that the argument would fail under scrutiny.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Socrates says 'consider these things from them to have been play for you' at 278c, then at 278c-d he announces 'after this it's clear that these two will display to you the serious things' and 'I will guide them so they pay back what they promised... they said they would display the protreptic wisdom.'
limits: This observation records the explicit play/serious transition and the framing of the next phase as a protreptic display; it does not assess whether the subsequent section actually fulfills the promise.
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates at 278c-d introduces a two-part argumentative procedure: first he will sketch his own understanding of what a protreptic display should be, then Euthydemus and Dionysodorus will exhibit theirs; he pre-emptively asks them not to laugh if his improvised sketch seems amateurish.
textual_basis: Socrates says 'I will indicate to you two what sort of thing I understand it to be and what sort of thing I desire to hear' (278d), then 'if I seem to do it amateurishly and laughably, don't laugh at me — out of eagerness to hear your wisdom I'll dare to improvise before you.'
limits: This observation records the procedure Socrates proposes and the self-deprecating hedge; it does not evaluate the content of the protreptic that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

He then says 'consider that you are now hearing the first parts of the sophistic sacred rites' (ta prota ton hieron akouein ton sophistikon)."

```yaml
feature_label: equivocation_manthanein_epistasthai
observation: Socrates diagnoses the brothers' first eristic question as exploiting an ambiguity in the verb meaning to learn or understand, which is applied both to the person who has no knowledge and is acquiring it, and to the person who already possesses knowledge and is using it to examine something.
textual_basis: At 278a-b Socrates explicitly says that the brothers have hidden the fact that the same name is used for opposite conditions: the one who knows and the one who does not. He spells out the two senses of the verb — acquiring knowledge initially and applying knowledge one already has.
limits: This observation records the diagnosis of equivocation by a speaker within the frame; it does not assess whether the diagnosis is correct or whether Plato endorses it. The same diagnosis applies to the second eristic question at 278b.
review_status: accepted
```

The remark is placed just before the narrator reports the sophist's next verbal trap against Cleinias."
```yaml
textual_basis: "At 276e, after Dionysodorus says all their questions are aphukta, Socrates replies: 'That's why you seem to me to have a good reputation among your pupils.' The juxtaposition of the boast about inescapable questions with the ironic remark about their reputation with pupils is made explicit by Socrates' toigar toi ('that's why')."
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0057
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 279b-279c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt

  text_sha256: b4093fe4619e07add8ba539b86e11d20444f5ae361a7ff87583d38fac2dea2d7
greek_terms: [εὐτυχίαν, σοφίαν]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1643
feature_label: goods_catalogue_compiled
observation: "Socrates leads Cleinias through a sequential catalogue of goods: bodily goods, noble birth, powers, honours, and the virtues (temperance, justice, courage). After each category, Cleinias assents that it is a good. Socrates then asks where to place wisdom (sophia) among the goods, and Cleinias places it among the goods. Finally Socrates claims they have omitted the greatest good — good fortune (eutychia) — which everyone, even the very base, calls the greatest of goods."
textual_basis: "At 279b Socrates enumerates bodily goods (kala ... kata to soma), noble birth (eugeneiai), powers (dynameis), and honours (timai), and asks whether the virtues — being temperate, just, and courageous — are goods, to which Cleinias assents. At 279c Socrates introduces wisdom (sophian) and asks where in the chorus of goods it should be placed; Cleinias places it among the goods. Socrates then recalls they have nearly omitted good fortune (eutychian), which all say is the greatest good."
limits: "This observation records the sequential enumeration of goods categories; it does not evaluate whether the catalogue is exhaustive, whether the ordering is significant, or whether Socrates' eventual argument will overturn the inclusion of good fortune."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0058

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "Socrates uses a series of short, directive questions to guide Cleinias through agreement on what counts as a good, without Cleinias initiating any items or challenging the procedure. The question form moves from assent-seeking ('did we think…?') to a placement question ('where in the chorus shall we place wisdom?') to a reflective prompt ('consider whether we are omitting anything of the goods')."
textual_basis: "At 279b Socrates asks 'ti oun … eti hemin leipetai ton agathon?' (what then is left for us of the goods?) and frames the question about the virtues with 'poteron pros Dios … hegei sy …?' (by Zeus, do you think…?). At 279c he asks 'ten de sophian pou chorou taxomen?' (where in the chorus shall we place wisdom?), shifting to a placement metaphor. He then prompts 'enthymou de me ti paraleipomen ton agathon' (consider whether we are omitting anything of the goods)."
limits: "This observation records the question-form and procedural control in this segment; it does not claim that Socrates is manipulating Cleinias or that the procedure would hold in a genuinely dialectical context."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0059
source_work: Euthydemus

greek_terms: [εὐτυχίαν, μέγιστον τῶν ἀγαθῶν]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1562
feature_label: socratic_feigned_ignorance
observation: "After Socrates and Cleinias agree they have omitted nothing, Socrates suddenly remembers, with an oath ('by Zeus'), that they are risking having omitted the greatest good — good fortune. He then attributes the claim that good fortune is the greatest good to everyone, even the very base, and seconds later catches himself as having nearly made themselves ridiculous. The sudden recollection, the attribution to common opinion, and the immediate self-correction dramatize a feigned discovery."
textual_basis: "At 279c, after Cleinias says they seem to have omitted nothing, Socrates says 'nai ma Dia kindyneuomen ge to megiston ton agathon paralipein' (by Zeus, we are risking having omitted the greatest of goods) and identifies it as eutychia, which 'pantes phasi, kai hoi pany phauloi, megiston ton agathon einai' (all say, even the very base, is the greatest of goods). He then immediately says 'oligou katagelastoi' (we nearly became ridiculous)."
limits: "This observation records the dramatic structure of the recollection and attribution to common opinion; it does not assess whether Socrates genuinely forgot or whether his eventual argument will subvert the inclusion of good fortune."
review_status: accepted
```

This establishes a step-by-step protreptic procedure.

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1101
feature_label: characteristic_catalogue_for_definiendum
observation: After securing assent that doing well requires many good things, Socrates begins assembling a catalogue of goods, starting with wealth (to ploutein) and health (to hugiainein), treating it as something any person could easily supply.
textual_basis: At 279a Socrates asks which things are goods (agatha de poia ara ton onton) and remarks it is not difficult for even an unimpressive man to list them; "everyone would say that being wealthy is a good" and then "also being healthy." The catalogue breaks off at the segment boundary.
limits: The record notes the start of an enumerative catalogue but does not assess whether the listed items are endorsed, provisional, or subverted later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - εὐηθέστερον
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: Socrates preemptively characterizes his own opening questions as laughable (katagelaston) and foolishly simple (euethestaton), while expecting the respondent and any listener to find the answers obvious, a pattern of mock self-deprecation that frames his procedure as naive.
textual_basis: At 278e he calls his first question "one of the laughable ones" (hen ton katagelaston), and at 279a he calls the follow-up "even more foolish than the first" (ekeinou eti euethestaton), each time knowing the answer is uncontroversial and getting immediate assent.
limits: This record identifies the irony marker; it does not claim that the content is actually foolish or that Socrates genuinely disavows the questions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates uses craftsmen (demiourgoi) and specifically the carpenter (tekton) as paradigmatic examples to argue that possessing materials is useless without the use (chresthai) that constitutes the craft activity.
textual_basis: At 280c Socrates says "all craftsmen, if all the materials needed for their work were prepared but they did not use them" and gives the carpenter as a specific example (280c-280d); Cleinias agrees that possession (ktesis) without use yields no benefit.
limits: The observation notes the craft analogy as a premise in the argument; it does not address whether crafts are the right comparison for the happiness argument that follows.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: After the analogical chain Socrates states the conclusion at 280d: "the one who is going to be happy must not only possess such goods but also use them; otherwise the possession yields no benefit." Cleinias agrees.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0066
source_work: Euthydemus

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: Socrates builds an assent chain at 280a through two parallel questions: whether one would rather share fortune with a wise or ignorant general, and whether one would rather risk illness with a wise or ignorant doctor. The respondent assents to the wise practitioner in each case, and Socrates draws the general conclusion that wisdom everywhere makes men fortunate.
textual_basis: The two questions are parallel in structure (metà sophoû … è metà amathoûs) and the respondent assents (sunechōrei). Socrates then states the general conclusion with ára (hē sophía ára pantachoû eutucheîn poieî toùs anthrōpous) and reinforces it with a self-evident claim that wisdom cannot err.
limits: This observation records the assent-chain structure and its craft-example content within 280a-280b. It does not claim that the argument is valid, nor does it address whether the conclusion about wisdom and good fortune is endorsed by Plato or by Socrates beyond the dramatic context.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1124
feature_label: recapitulation_or_restart
observation: At 280b Socrates recapitulates the conclusion just reached ("when wisdom is present, he who has it needs nothing further of good fortune") and then explicitly returns to prior agreements for further examination, asking how the earlier admissions about possessing many goods and happiness would stand given the new conclusion.
textual_basis: Socrates marks the recapitulation with sunōmologēsámetha teleutôntes ("we finally agreed in sum"), states the summary conclusion in a compressed formula, and then signals the return to prior material with pálin epunthanómēn autoû tà próteron hōmologēména pôs àn hēmîn échoi ("I again inquired of him how the things previously agreed would hold for us").
limits: This observation records the structural move of recapitulation and return within the argumentative procedure at 280b. It does not evaluate whether the recapitulation is accurate or whether the return to prior agreements is logically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0071
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 281d-281e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 281d-281e
  start_marker: 281d
  end_marker: 281e
  start_char: 21787
  end_char: 22514
  text_sha256: c984ba3e7619102a7d35b09f17e407261037bbe1d026cd15916072bdb0aa67b7
greek_terms: ["φρόνησίς", "σοφία", "ἀμαθία"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_802
feature_label: evaluative_term_revaluation
observation: |
  Socrates recapitulates and revises the evaluation of conventional goods by making their value wholly dependent on whether they are led by wisdom (phronesis/sophia) or ignorance (amathia). What were initially called goods are now said to be neither good nor bad in themselves (auta de kath' hauta oudetera auton oudenos axia einai).
textual_basis: |
  At 281d Socrates says that everything they initially called goods is now at risk of not being good in itself, but rather greater goods if led by phronesis and sophia, and greater evils if led by amathia — the goods themselves being worth nothing in themselves. At 281e he draws the conclusion: of all things, nothing else is either good or bad, and of these two, sophia alone is good and amathia alone bad. Clinias explicitly agrees (homologei).
limits: |
  This observation records the revaluation of goods and the categorical claim that wisdom alone is good. It does not assess whether this is a stable Socratic position across dialogues or whether the argument for it is sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0072

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1124
feature_label: recapitulation_or_restart
observation: |
  Socrates marks a summary turning-point with the phrase "in summary" (en kephalaioi), then poses a question about what follows from what has been said (ti oun hemin symbainei ek ton eiremenon). The summary condenses the prior discussion of goods and reorients the inquiry toward a new conclusion.
textual_basis: |
  At 281d Socrates uses en kephalaioi to introduce the summative claim about goods being neither good nor bad in themselves. Immediately afterward at 281e he asks ti oun hemin symbainei ek ton eiremenon and draws the conclusion that sophia is the only good and amathia the only evil. These are explicit structural markers of recapitulation and logical transition.
limits: |
  This records the presence of recapitulation and transition markers; it does not assess whether the summary fairly represents the preceding argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_522
feature_label: definition_established_by_assent_chain
observation: |
  Socrates draws a conclusion from prior agreements and Clinias assents to it step by step. The definitional claim that wisdom alone is good and ignorance alone is bad is established through an assent chain rather than independent argument: Socrates poses the conclusion as a question (allo ti e...) and Clinias agrees (homologei).
textual_basis: |
  At 281e Socrates explicitly derives the conclusion from what has been said (ek ton eiremenon) and frames it as a question inviting Clinias to confirm: "is it not that of other things none is either good or bad, and of these two, wisdom is good and ignorance is bad?" Clinias's agreement (homologei) completes the assent chain.
limits: |
  This records that the definition is established through sequential agreement. It does not evaluate whether the prior premises are sufficient or whether Clinias fully understands what he is assenting to.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates asserts at 282a-b that it is not shameful to beg, supplicate, serve, and be a slave to a lover or anyone, willing to perform any fine service, for the sake of obtaining wisdom."
textual_basis: "Socrates states it is not at all shameful to beg and supplicate for a share of wisdom, and not blameworthy to serve and be a slave to a lover or anyone. This inverts the conventional Athenian ethical valuation of servitude and supplication as degrading, reframing them as honorable when the goal is wisdom."
limits: "Records the inversion of conventional shame around servitude; does not claim this is a general Socratic doctrine or that the argument is logically derived rather than rhetorically asserted."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: 4c7eb61fe1a0f124899d1ceae257ed64cba174712c7d532590260dd2170daa55
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_425
feature_label: reductio_conclusion
observation: >
  Euthydemus draws a reductio conclusion from the agreed premise that the
  interlocutors want Cleinias to become wise and not be ignorant (283d). Since
  they want him to become what he is not and no longer be what he is now,
  Euthydemus infers that they want him to perish (283d). The narrator reports
  his own disturbance at hearing this (283d).
textual_basis: >
  At 283d Euthydemus asks whether the interlocutors want Cleinias to become wise
  and not be ignorant; they agree. He then argues that they want him to become
  what he is not and cease to be what he is now, concluding that they want him
  to perish. The narrator (Socrates) explicitly says he was disturbed by the
  argument (katharã³ ethorybēthēn, 283d).
limits: >
  The observation records the reductio structure and the narrator's reaction; it
  does not claim that Euthydemus intends this seriously or that the argument is
  valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Ctesippus reacts with indignation and near-abusive language toward Euthydemus

  perish (283d-283e). Ctesippus says that if it were not too crude to say, he
  would direct an imprecation at Euthydemus, and he accuses Euthydemus of
  falsely attributing to him an unholy wish (283e).
textual_basis: >
  At 283e Ctesippus is described as indignant on behalf of his beloved
  (ēganaktēsen te hyper tōn paidikōn). He says that were it not too crude, he
  would say a curse against Euthydemus, and he rejects the claim that he would
  want Cleinias to perish as not even holy to say (283e).
limits: >

  or whether Euthydemus intended provocation. It records only the character
  reaction and the terms of the charge.
```

```yaml
  Socrates frames the inquiry as a question of whether the young man should acquire all knowledge or whether there is a single knowledge that brings happiness and makes a man good. textual_basis: |
  At 282e Socrates states that he left off at the question whether the young man must acquire all knowledge or whether there is one knowledge to obtain in order to be happy and good. He explicitly identifies this as the next step for the brothers' exhibition.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "Dionysodorus tells Socrates at 283c to see that he does not become a denier of what he is now saying. Socrates replies that he has considered it and will never become a denier. The exchange secures Socrates' commitment before the eristic refutation sequence begins."
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0097

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1935
feature_label: respondent_objection_with_distinction
observation: "Ctesippus partially concedes the conclusion that Dionysodorus speaks 'what is' (ta onta) but immediately objects by drawing a distinction: Dionysodorus speaks what is 'in a certain way' (tropon tina) but not 'as it is' (hōs ge echei). This shifts the dispute from the truth of reference to the correctness of the mode of speaking."
textual_basis: "At 284c Ctesippus says: 'by Zeus, Euthydemus, but he speaks the things that are (ta onta) in a certain way (tropon tina), yet not, however, as they are (ou mentoi hōs ge echei).' This half-concession distinguishes between speaking about real things and speaking them correctly, a distinction the sophist does not concede."
limits: "This observation does not evaluate whether the distinction Ctesippus draws is philosophically adequate; it only records that a respondent interrupts the chain of concessions with a modal distinction about how something is said."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0098
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 284c-284d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 284c-284d
  start_marker: 284c
  end_marker: 284d
  start_char: 27604
  end_char: 28519
  text_sha256: c828823074dfcb4e8b43278c7356dbae659f1113182b6b65778f403d1a93ca59
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1936
feature_label: homonymy_exploitation
observation: "Dionysodorus exploits an ambiguity in the phrase 'as they are' (hōs echei) by taking the adverbial sense of the qualifier 'well' or 'badly' (eu/kakōs) and applying it to the verb 'speak': if good men speak things as they are, and bad things are badly, then good men speak badly when they speak of bad things. The equivocation collapses an ontological qualifier into a manner-of-speaking adverb."
textual_basis: "At 284d Dionysodorus asks Ctesippus whether good things are well (eu) and bad things badly (kakōs), and Ctesippus concedes. Then Dionysodorus asks whether 'the beautiful and good' speak things as they are (hōs echei), and Ctesippus agrees. Dionysodorus concludes: 'Then the good speak badly (kakōs), Ctesippus, concerning bad things, if indeed they speak them as they are (hōs echei).' The slide from 'bad things are badly off' to 'good men speak badly' depends on treating 'badly' as an adverb modifying 'speak' rather than as a state of the subject matter."
limits: "This observation only records the mechanism of the equivocation in the text; it does not assess whether Plato endorses or rejects the ambiguity as philosophically revealing."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 285e Dionysodorus asks 'Would you also undertake to give an account of this?' (e kai huposchois an toutou logon;) and Ctesippus replies 'Absolutely' (panu). This sets up an elenctic exchange about whether contradicting exists.
limits: The observation records the procedural move, not the outcome of the subsequent argument about the existence of contradiction.
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_380
feature_label: forced_alternative
observation: Dionysodorus opens the elenctic examination at 285e by posing a forced alternative: for each being there are accounts, and those accounts state the thing either as it is or as it is not.
textual_basis: At 285e the exchange begins: 'Are there accounts for each of the things that are?' (eisin hekasto ton onton logoi;) — 'Certainly.' — 'And are they as each thing is or as it is not?' (oukoun hos estin hekaston e hos ouk estin;). The question frames the domain of contradiction as exhausted by two exclusive options.
limits: This observation records the binary framing device at the start of the argument; it does not assess whether the dichotomy is genuine or sophistical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: turn_geometry

feature_label: adverbial_play_escalation
observation: >-
  Ctesippus escalates Euthydemus's adverbial wordplay by extending the pattern
  from "good men speak ill of the bad" to parallel pairs (cold/coldly,
  hot/hotly, big/greatly), culminating in the claim that the cold both speak
  and converse coldly (284e).
textual_basis: >-
  Euthydemus asks whether the good speak of the great greatly (megalos) and the
  hot hotly (thermos). Ctesippus affirms and adds the cold speaking coldly
  (psychrous psychros) and saying they converse coldly, extending the
  adverbial pattern beyond Euthydemus's initial move.
limits: >-
  Does not establish that this is eristic rather than playful banter; the
  passage is framed within a larger contest of one-upmanship whose stakes are
  not fully clear from this segment alone.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  (noutheto), claiming affection (philo) for Dionysodorus and the status of
  comrades (hetairon) (284e).
textual_basis: >-
  When Dionysodorus says "you abuse, Ctesippus, you abuse" (loidorei, o
  Ktesippe, loidorei), Ctesippus replies "by Zeus, not I" and immediately
  substitutes the vocabulary of friendly counsel: noutheto, philo, hetairon.
limits: >-

  correction to regain conversational footing.
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates draws the absurd consequence that if false speaking is impossible, then ignorance and ignorant people do not exist either."
textual_basis: "At 286c Dionysodorus defends the Protagorean thesis that false speaking is impossible. Socrates at 286d extends this by assent chain: if false speaking is impossible, then neither is false opinion possible; if false opinion is impossible, then ignorance and ignorant people cannot exist. Dionysodorus assents at each step."
limits: "The observation records the reductio movement, not whether Socrates endorses the conclusion or considers it genuinely absurd. The argument is presented as exposing the consequence of Dionysodorus' position."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: |
  Socrates intervenes to tell Ctesippus that the brothers' wisdom is marvelous and that they are not refusing to display it seriously but are imitating Proteus the Egyptian sophist and enchanting them (288b). This praise is embedded in a management move to defuse Ctesippus's hostility.
textual_basis: |
  At 288b Socrates says the brothers' wisdom is marvelous (thaumasia), and that they are imitating Proteus the Egyptian sophist and enchanting (goeteuonte) them. The Proteus comparison and the claim of enchantment frame the brothers' eristic behavior as a deliberate, almost magical performance rather than a failure to argue seriously.
limits: |
  Whether Socrates' attribution of marvelous wisdom is sincere or ironic is not determined here. The observation records the form of praise and the Proteus-enchantment comparison used to manage the dramatic situation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: dd2ed4a00b838ad456de68ee9b0e6df0df60409d30c3bc29a540fbfaf805091c
greek_terms:
  - ἐξηλέγξαμεν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1332
feature_label: prior_concession_recalled_for_refutation
observation: "Socrates explicitly recalls a previously established refutation to constrain the current inquiry about which knowledge is beneficial."
textual_basis: "At 288e-289a, Socrates says 'but the earlier point we refuted' (to proteron... touto ge exêlenxamen) and restates that even unlimited gold without the knowledge of how to use it was shown to be of no advantage, before extending the point to all productive crafts."
limits: "This is a procedural recall of a prior elenctic result, not itself a new refutation of a fresh claim."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ποιεῖν

english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_1192
feature_label: user_maker_knowledge_distinction
observation: "Socrates argues that productive crafts are worthless for happiness unless paired with knowledge of how to use what they produce, enumerating money-making, medicine, and any other craft that knows how to make but not how to use its product."
textual_basis: "At 288e-289a, Socrates lists chrêmatistikês, iatrikês, and any other craft (allês oudemias) that knows how to make something (poiein) but not how to use (chrêsthai) what it makes, concluding none of these provides benefit."
limits: "The worthlessness claim is conditional on the premise that the knowledge sought must be beneficial for eudaimonia; it does not deny these crafts have instrumental value in other domains."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0121
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 289b-289c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt

  start_marker: "289b"
  end_marker: "289c"
  start_char: 37810
  end_char: 38657
  text_sha256: "6af38c63f46eb824bd69e20229d65812223cdfdcefe51fff5139bee623307869"
greek_terms: ["ποιούσα τέχνη", "χρωμένη", "λυροποιικὴ", "κιθαριστικὴ"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_2081
feature_label: craft_production_use_separation
observation: Socrates argues that lyre-making (289b-c) and flute-making (289c) are not the knowledge sought because in each case the producing craft and the using craft are separate, while the sought knowledge must combine both functions.
textual_basis: At 289b-289c, Socrates states that the lyre-making craft and the lyre-playing craft differ greatly from each other, and flute-making is the same kind of case. The criterion is that the desired knowledge must be one in which making and knowing-how-to-use coincide.
limits: This observation identifies the craft structure used to eliminate candidate knowledges. It does not determine what the correct knowledge is or whether the production/use criterion is itself valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: ["συνεδόκει", "συνέφη", "λογοποιικὴν τέχνην"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2082
feature_label: serial_craft_elimination
observation: Socrates eliminates three candidate crafts in rapid succession at 289b-289c: lyre-making, flute-making, and speech-making, each rejected by Cleinias' assent.
textual_basis: At 289b-c, after establishing agreement that the knowledge sought must combine making and using, Socrates asks whether they need lyre-makers, answered no. Flute-making is similarly dismissed at 289c. Then speech-making is rejected by Cleinias at 289c. Each elimination uses the same structural criterion.
limits: This observation notes the serial elimination pattern. It does not assess whether the eliminations are valid or whether speech-making deserves the same rejection as the instrumental crafts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 289b-289c, a definitional criterion for the knowledge that produces happiness is stated as one in which making and knowing-how-to-use coincide, and this criterion is then used to eliminate lyre-making, flute-making, and speech-making as candidates.
textual_basis: At 289b, Socrates says the knowledge sought must be one in which making and knowing-how-to-use what one makes coincide. At 289b-289c, lyre-making and flute-making are eliminated because their making and using arts are separate. Speech-making is rejected at 289c, extending the ladder of eliminated candidates.
limits: This observation identifies the criterion and its application. It does not determine whether the criterion is correct or whether speech-making is properly eliminated by it.
```

```yaml
  - σοφῶν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: Socrates calls the sophists' arguments difficult and says this is reasonable since they come from wise men, then immediately asks Dionysodorus to explain what his phrase means.
textual_basis: At 287b Socrates states that the arguments are difficult and that this is reasonable because they are spoken by wise men, then at 287c he asks Dionysodorus to explain the phrase that he had just claimed is especially hard to use. The surface deference to wisdom is combined with a demand for clarification that exposes the unintelligibility of the claimed wisdom.
limits: The passage contains surface deference to sophists' wisdom; this observation does not claim Socrates holds a hidden view about their ignorance. The deferential language is in the text; the contrast with the request for clarification is also in the text.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0128
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 290c-290d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 290c-290d
  start_marker: 290c
  end_marker: 290d
  start_char: 40358
  end_char: 41253
  text_sha256: 6c8e16c808259647f7625427ea1cc501a342ed600ce7e21a8723036074bb2a4c
greek_terms:
  - θηρευτικοί
  - ὀρτυγοθῆραι
  - ὀρτυγοτρόφοις
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1961
feature_label: hunting_metaphor_for_inquiry
observation: >
  The hunting-versus-using distinction is applied across three domains in
  sequence: geometers/astronomers/calculators hunt (discover) what exists
  and hand over to dialecticians; generals hunt a city or camp and hand
  over to politicians; quail-hunters hand over to quail-keepers. All of
  these hunting crafts lack the knowledge of how to use what they acquire.
textual_basis: >
  At 290c the geometers, astronomers, and calculators are called hunters
  because they discover rather than produce their diagrams, and they hand
  over their findings to dialecticians. At 290c-d the same pattern is
  applied to generals who capture a city or camp and hand over to
  politicians, explicitly compared to quail-hunters handing over to
  quail-keepers. The conclusion at 290d states the need for a craft that
  knows how to use what it acquires.
limits: >
  This observation records the extension of the hunting/using distinction
  across domains; it does not evaluate whether the analogy between
  intellectual discovery and literal hunting is valid or whether the
  handoff model accurately describes these crafts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Socrates tests Dionysodorus' claim of universal knowledge by asking whether he and Euthydemus know specific crafts (carpentry, leatherworking, shoe-repair) and empirical facts (the number of stars and grains of sand). Dionysodorus affirms all of them without hesitation.
textual_basis: |
  At 294b Socrates asks whether the brothers know tektonike (carpentry) and skutike (leatherworking), then whether they are capable of neuro-rhaphein (stitching leather) and kattuein (mending shoes). He then asks about the number of stars and grains of sand. Dionysodorus affirms each, adding "by Zeus, even mending shoes." The sequence tests the scope of the universal-knowledge claim against concrete craft and empirical domains.
limits: |
  Does not evaluate whether the craft-test refutes the claim; only records that the narrowing pattern is present. Does not claim that Socrates believes the answers are sincere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: "hunting me by setting names around me"
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: "Socrates narrates that he recognised Euthydemus was annoyed with him for distinguishing what was said, and that Euthydemus wanted to hunt him by surrounding him with names (295d). Socrates then recalls his cithara teacher Connus, who also gets irritated when Socrates does not yield, and reduces his attention to him as a slow learner. On this basis Socrates decides he must yield to Euthydemus too, for fear of being rejected as a pupil, and defers to Euthydemus's superior expertise in dialectic at 295e."
textual_basis: "Socrates frames his concession as motivated by fear of being rejected as a pupil, explicitly stating that Euthydemus 'knows better how to converse' since he has a craft (techne) while Socrates is a layman (idiotes). The comparison with Connus, a music teacher, sets up an ironic parallel between craft instruction and the sophist's verbal trapping."
limits: "The observation identifies the mock-deference structure (yielding under threat, exaggerated praise of expertise, comparison to a craft teacher). It does not settle whether Socrates' deference is sincere or strategic, nor does it evaluate Euthydemus's actual dialectical skill."
review_status: accepted
```

Socrates observes that he cannot know how to proceed unless he first understands what is being asked."
```yaml
textual_basis: "At 295b Dionysodorus insists on a pure respondent role (hos apokrinoumenou erota), then at 295c Socrates negotiates the terms: he will conform to whatever procedure Dionysodorus commands, but questions whether he must answer even when he does not know what is being asked. The constraint is procedural rather than substantive."
limits: "This shows a procedural power struggle at the start of an elenctic exchange, not the outcome of any logical refutation. It does not demonstrate that Socrates is unable to answer on substantive grounds."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "Dionysodorus tries to bind Socrates to answer by claiming that Socrates already has some supposition (hupolambaneis) about what is meant. He instructs Socrates to answer 'toward what you suppose.' Socrates resists by observing that his supposition may differ from what Dionysodorus intends in asking, and finally refuses to answer (ma Dia) until he has first learned what is being asked."
textual_basis: "295c: Dionysodorus says 'you surely suppose something of what I am saying' and instructs Socrates to answer toward that supposition. Socrates responds hypothetically: what if you ask with one meaning but I suppose another? Dionysodorus grants that would be acceptable for him but not for Socrates, and Socrates then refuses to answer until he finds out."
limits: "This is a procedural negotiation about the terms of questioning and answering, not a refutation of any thesis. It does not settle whether the Socratic or eristic procedure should govern."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0158
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 293b-293c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 293b-293c
  start_marker: 293b
  end_marker: 293c
  start_char: 46028
  end_char: 46919
  text_sha256: d510b3ef0cd62761e368df0a2935c0991f532ed77ee13eb9ef07009318f091b1
greek_terms:
  - ἐπίστασαι
  - ἐπιστήμων
  - ἐπίστασθαι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2302
feature_label: eristic_predicate_scope_equivocation
observation: >
  The sophist exploits the ambiguity between knowing some particular thing and
  being epistemon (knowledgeable) simpliciter. At 293b-c Socrates is led from
  the admission that he knows something (epistasai ti) to the claim that he
  is epistemon, from which the sophist infers he must know everything.
  When Socrates objects that there are many things he does not know, the
  sophist retorts that if he does not know something he is not epistemon,
  contradicting his earlier admission that he is epistemon.
textual_basis: >
  The sequence runs: "do you know anything?" (esti hoti epistasai), Socrates
  agrees; "therefore you are epistemon" (epistemon ei); "mustn't you know
  everything if you are epistemon?" (ananke se echein panta epistasthai
  epistemon ge onta). Socrates objects that he does not know many things;
  the sophist replies "if you do not know something, you are not epistemon"
  (ei ti me epistasai, ouk epistemon ei), then notes the contradiction with
  Socrates' earlier admission. The equivocation turns on whether epistemon
  takes an object (knowledgeable of X) or stands as an unqualified predicate.
limits: >
  This observation identifies the logical mechanism of the eristic trap; it
  does not assess whether the sophist's argument is valid or whether Socrates
  could have escaped it by a different response.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  The sophist at 293b-c drives a rapid series of short questions each
  answered by Socrates with minimal assent, building a chain that ends in an
  apparent contradiction. Socrates is made to affirm both that he is
  epistemon and that he is not epistemon.
textual_basis: >
  The question-answer chain begins with "phere de moi apokrinai" and proceeds
  through a sequence of questions each receiving brief assent (panu ge,
  egoge) until the contradiction is reached at 293c: Socrates is said to have
  claimed both that he is epistemon (arti de epistemon ephestha einai) and
  that he is not.
limits: >
  This observation records the formal structure of the exchange; it does not
  evaluate whether the contradiction is genuine or merely verbal.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - διδάξω
  - ἐπιδείξω
  - ἔχοντα
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: >
  At 293b the sophist offers Socrates a choice between two modes of
  proceeding: he will either teach (didaxo) Socrates the knowledge in
  question or demonstrate (epideixo) that Socrates already possesses it.
  Socrates chooses the demonstration, citing his age as making demonstration
  easier than learning.
textual_basis: >
  The sophist opens with "shall I teach you this knowledge...or demonstrate
  that you have it?" (poteron...didaxo e epideixo echonta). Socrates replies
  "demonstrate that I have it, for that is much easier than learning for a
  man of this age" (epideixon...echonta; polu gar rhaon e manthanein
  telikonde andra).
limits: >
  This observation records the epideixis frame as a turn-structuring device;
  it does not assess whether the demonstration is genuine or sophistical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  The narrator explicitly marks that Dionysodorus quickly intercepts the

  Dionysodorus took over rapidly so that Ctesippus would not say anything
  beforehand (298e).
textual_basis: >
  The clause "hina mē proteron ti eipoi ho Ktēsippos" (298e) makes the
  purpose of the interception explicit: the narrator describes the
  procedural move as a deliberate turn-seizure aimed at denying Ctesippus
  a chance to respond before the next sophism lands.
limits: >
  This observation notes the marked procedural nature of the turn-taking
  but does not assess whether Dionysodorus's new question follows fairly
  from the preceding exchange. The remark is about dramatic staging, not
  about the logical validity of the argument that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0162
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 298e-299a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 298e-299a
  start_marker: 298e
  end_marker: 299a
  start_char: 58168
  end_char: 59111
  text_sha256: 5044c954491878a5b849bb1977109eef595ac63c7fa4acca6653a97ce6117821
greek_terms:
  - σὸς πατὴρ γίγνεται ὁ κύων
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2333
feature_label: equivocation_sophism
observation: >
  Dionysodorus constructs an argument chain (298e) that concludes the dog
  is Ctesippus's father and Ctesippus is the brother of puppies. The
  argument exploits an equivocation: the dog belongs to Ctesippus in the
  sense of ownership, and the dog is a father in the sense of having sired
  puppies; the conclusion treats the ownership relation as if it were a
  kinship relation.
textual_basis: >
  The chain proceeds: Ctesippus has a dog, the dog fathered puppies,
  therefore the dog is a father. Since the dog is Ctesippus's (
  ownership), the dog who is a father is Ctesippus's, yielding "the dog
  becomes your father" (298e). The shift from possession to kinship is
  the mechanism that produces the absurd conclusion.
limits: >
  The observation identifies the equivocation structure but does not claim
  that all elenctic arguments in the dialogue use this device, nor does it
  assess whether Dionysodorus intends the equivocation or presents it as a
  serious demonstration.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_2334
feature_label: laughter_as_ironic_deflection
observation: >
  Ctesippus responds to Dionysodorus's aggressive questioning with
  laughter and an oath: he laughs, swears by the gods, and says he cannot
  strike Dionysodorus (298e). The laugh and oath mark an ironic refusal to
  engage the sophism on its own terms, deflecting the question about
  striking the dog with a quip about striking the questioner instead.
textual_basis: >
  The text places the laugh ("gelasas") immediately before the oath
  ("nē tous theous") and the reply "ou gar dunamai se" (298e). The
  rapid sequence of laugh, oath, and ambiguous refusal signals ironic
  distance rather than straightforward assent or defeat.
limits: >
  The observation records the dramatic markers of irony but does not
  determine whether Ctesippus genuinely finds the argument amusing or is
  using laughter as a defensive tactic. It also does not claim that every
  instance of laughter in the dialogue carries the same ironic function.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: premise_pushed_to_absurd_consequence
observation: "Euthydemus secures Ctesippus's agreement that gold is a good thing and that good things should always and everywhere be possessed, then pushes the premise to the absurd conclusion that the happiest man would have three talents of gold in his belly, a talent in his skull, and a gold stater in each eye (299d-299e)."
textual_basis: "The chain proceeds from 'is gold a good thing to have?' through 'shouldn't good things always and everywhere be had?' to the literal-body-containment conclusion, which no interlocutor would accept as a serious proposal for happiness."
limits: "This observation records the argument structure; it does not determine whether the move is sincere eristic reasoning or deliberate parody, nor whether Euthydemus expects Ctesippus to accept the conclusion."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: eristic_contradiction_trap
observation: "Euthydemus attempts to trap Socrates in a simultaneous knowing-and-not-knowing contradiction by manipulating Socrates's question about whether good men are unjust. When Dionysodorus answers that Socrates never learned that good men are unjust (297a), Euthydemus objects that this will make Socrates appear both knowing and not-knowing."
textual_basis: "At 296e Socrates asks Euthydemus whether he knows that good men are unjust. At 297a Euthydemus says Socrates knows that good men are NOT unjust, changing the predicate. When Dionysodorus concedes Socrates never learned the affirmative version, Euthydemus says Dionysodorus is ruining the argument because Socrates will now appear not to know, being simultaneously knowing and ignorant (epistemon hama on kai anepistemon)."
limits: "This observation records the eristic trap structure. It does not assess whether the contradiction is genuine or merely verbal, nor does it claim that Socrates is actually trapped."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0170

feature_label: eristic_predicate_scope_equivocation
observation: "Euthydemus exploits the ambiguity between relational and non-relational predication of 'father' at 298c. He asks whether his father is only his father or also the father of other men, treating a relational predicate ('father of X') as though it were a simple non-relational predicate. When Ctesippus objects, Euthydemus reinforces the equivocation by parallel cases: 'being gold not to be gold? or being a human not to be human?' (298c)."
textual_basis: "At 298c Euthydemus asks whether his father is only his or also of other men. The possessive marks the relational sense, but Euthydemus immediately shifts to the bare predicate 'to be a father,' treating the relational property as a non-relational attribute. He then extends the pattern to gold and human as parallel cases, reinforcing the equivocation."
limits: "This observation notes only the equivocation structure in 298c-298d. It does not claim that Plato endorses or rejects this sophistical move, nor does it assess the logical validity of the argument."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Ctesippus responds to Euthydemus' equivocation by extending it to absurd animal conclusions at 298c-298d: if Euthydemus' mother is mother of all, then she is mother of sea urchins; if he is brother of all, then he is brother of gudgeons, puppies, and piglets; and his father is a boar and a dog. This reductio ad absurdum turns the sophist's own equivocation against him."
textual_basis: "At 298c-d Ctesippus systematically extends the predicate from human relatives to animals: first horses and other animals (298c), then sea urchins (298d), then small fish, puppies, and piglets (298d), culminating in boar and dog (298d). Each step follows from the prior concession that Euthydemus' parents are parents 'of all' (298d). The progression from human to increasingly absurd animal categories exhibits the reductio structure."
limits: "This observation records Ctesippus' reductio strategy at 298c-298d. It does not claim that Ctesippus successfully refutes Euthydemus, nor does it assess whether the reductio is logically sound."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: equivocation_sophism
observation: Dionysodorus exploits Socrates' use of the verb for "is present in" regarding beauty at 301a, shifting to the verb for "come to be present with" to produce absurd consequences: if a cow comes to be present with you, you become a cow; if Dionysodorus is present with you, you are Dionysodorus.
textual_basis: Socrates says beauty is present in each beautiful thing. Dionysodorus immediately pivots: if a cow comes to be present with you, are you a cow? And because I am now present with you, are you Dionysodorus? He then generalizes the trap at the end of 301a: in what way, when one thing comes to be present with another, would the one be the other?
limits: This records the equivocation on presence/present-with. It does not evaluate whether the sophism is valid or characterize Dionysodorus' broader method.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2302
feature_label: eristic_predicate_scope_equivocation
observation: >
  Dionysodorus extends Socrates' admission that it is fitting for a butcher to
  cut and skin to the conclusion that slaughtering and butchering the butcher
  himself is doing what is fitting, and extends the same pattern to the
  bronzeworker and potter (301d-301e).
textual_basis: >
  Socrates agrees that what is fitting (prosēkonta) for a butcher is to cut and
  skin. Dionysodorus then treats the butcher not as agent but as patient of the
  same action, and draws the absurd conclusion that butchering the butcher is
  doing what is fitting. He then applies the same eristic move to the
  bronzeworker (chalkeus) and potter (kerameus).
limits: >
  This observation records the eristic equivocation pattern; it does not claim
  that Dionysodorus believes the conclusion or that the argument is intended to
  be taken seriously.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: >
  At 301d-301e Socrates exclaims "O Poseidon" and tells Dionysodorus he is
  putting the finishing touch (kolophōna) on his wisdom, then wonders whether
  this wisdom will ever become his own.
textual_basis: >
  Socrates' exclamation directly follows Dionysodorus' absurd extension of the
  craft-domain argument to butchering the butcher. Calling this eristic move a
  "crowning achievement of wisdom" (kolophōna … tē sophia) and then wondering
  whether he will ever possess such wisdom is manifestly ironic, since the
  argument is patently absurd.
limits: >
  This observation records the presence of ironic praise in the text; it does
  not claim that Socrates intends to teach or that the sophists are aware of
  the irony.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0183
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 298a-298b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 298a-298b
  start_marker: 298a
  end_marker: 298b
  start_char: 56369
  end_char: 57259
  text_sha256: "c74813169f2c110b465d127a6bd74032b918c11afb738240f4fe46153114110a"
greek_terms:
  - πατήρ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2302
feature_label: eristic_predicate_scope_equivocation
observation: >
  Dionysodorus constructs a valid inference pattern in 298a (different from a stone entails not a stone; different from gold entails not gold), then applies the same form to the relational predicate "father." Chaeredemus is different from Socrates' father (Sophroniscus), therefore Chaeredemus is declared not a father. The argument equivocates on the scope of "father," shifting from "father of Socrates" (relational) to "father" simpliciter (absolute).
textual_basis: >
  At 298a Dionysodorus secures Socrates' assent that being different from a stone makes one not a stone, and different from gold makes one not gold. He then asks whether Chaeredemus is different from Socrates' father (Sophroniscus), and when Socrates agrees, concludes that Chaeredemus is not a father. The valid pattern with non-relational predicates is illicitly transferred to the relational predicate "father."
limits: >
  This observation identifies the formal structure of the equivocation. It does not claim that the sophists are aware of the fallacy, nor does it assess whether Socrates' assent was forced or voluntary.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_346
feature_label: inversion_counterargument
observation: >
  Ctesippus attempts at 298b to turn the eristic argument back on Euthydemus by asking whether Euthydemus' own father is different from Ctesippus' father, which would by the same logic render Euthydemus fatherless. Euthydemus blocks the inversion by denying the factual premise, claiming his father is the same as Ctesippus' father.
textual_basis: >
  At 298b Ctesippus interrupts to ask whether Euthydemus' father has suffered the same fate — that is, whether he is different from Ctesippus' father. Euthydemus denies this ("far from it") and claims the fathers are the same, which Ctesippus finds unwelcome.
limits: >
  This observation records the attempted inversion and its blockage. It does not assess whether Ctesippus' countermove is logically sound or whether Euthydemus' denial is sincere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0185

greek_terms:
  - ἀδελφός
  - ὁμομήτριος
  - ὁμοπάτριος
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2392
feature_label: equivocation_contradiction
observation: Dionysodorus exploits the fact that Patrocles is Socrates' half-brother (same mother, different father) to produce an eristic contradiction: Patrocles is Socrates' brother and not his brother. When Socrates clarifies the half-blood relation by specifying different fathers (Sophroniscus vs. Chaeredemus), Dionysodorus pivots to a further equivocation on 'father' itself, asking whether Sophroniscus and Chaeredemus were both fathers.
textual_basis: At 297e, Socrates states that Patrocles is his brother by the same mother but not the same father. Dionysodorus immediately concludes 'so he is your brother and not your brother.' When Socrates resists by naming the two different fathers, Dionysodorus asks 'Was Sophroniscus a father, and Chaeredemus?' — collapsing the distinction between being a father of different sons into an apparent identity question.
limits: This observation identifies an eristic technique of equivocation; it does not claim that the argument is formally valid or that Socrates is refuted in substance. It also does not assert that all eristic in the dialogue operates the same way.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 297d, Socrates states that Dionysodorus will not stop questioning, and does so out of envy and to hinder, so that Euthydemus may not teach him that wisdom. This directly characterizes the interrogator's motives and the procedural pressure of the eristic exchange.
limits: This observation notes the explicit procedural complaint; it does not establish whether the complaint is sincere or ironic, nor does it evaluate the fairness of the characterization.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 297e, Socrates states Patrocles is his brother by the same mother but not the same father; Sophroniscus is his father, Chaeredemus is Patrocles' father. He clarifies that Iphicles (Heracles' brother) is not the same as Patrocles, though the names are similar.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0188
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 302e-303a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 302e-303a
  start_marker: 302e
  end_marker: 303a
  start_char: 66816
  end_char: 67631
  text_sha256: 10c0f51e07252453b6f9636b789cff89797a1d408e44977e8a76236988eab5e0
greek_terms: ["ψυχήν", "ζῷα", "θεοί", "ἀνάδυσις"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: >
  Euthydemus constructs a chain that traps Socrates: all things with soul are
  living beings (302e), gods have souls, so gods are living beings. He then
  applies Socrates' prior admission that the living beings that are his are
  those he may give away, sell, or sacrifice, yielding the absurd consequence
  that Socrates can sell Zeus and the other gods (303a).
textual_basis: >
  Euthydemus explicitly recites the linked admissions: "you agreed that
  whatever has soul is a living being. Or do these gods not have soul?" At 302e
  Socrates concedes each step (gods have soul, gods are living beings), and
  then Euthydemus reintroduces the earlier premise about ownership of living
  beings (ability to give, sell, sacrifice). At 303a he asks whether Socrates
  may sell or give away Zeus and the other gods. Socrates marks the trap with
  "there is no escape for me, Euthydemus" (ouk estin gar moi anadusis) at 302e.
limits: >
  This observation records the structure of the eristic chain but does not
  assess whether the prior admissions were made under the same conditions or
  whether the term "living being" (zoon) is used univocally across the
  admissions. It does not claim that Socrates is genuinely refuted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0189

greek_terms: ["πυππάξ", "Ἡρακλῆς"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2396
feature_label: exclamation_treated_as_predication
observation: >
  When Ctesippus exclaims "Puppax, O Heracles, a fine argument!" Dionysodorus
  immediately asks whether Heracles is puppax or puppax is Heracles, treating
  the idiomatic exclamation as a logical predication and generating an eristic
  puzzle from it.
textual_basis: >
  At 303a Ctesippus says "Puppax, O Heracles, a fine argument" (Puppax o
  Herakleis, kalou logou). Dionysodorus responds immediately: "Is Heracles
  puppax, or is puppax Heracles?" (poteron oun ho Herakles puppax estin e ho
  puppax Herakles). The move converts an idiomatic interjection into a subject-
  predicate question, treating puppax as a predicate applicable to Heracles.
limits: >
  This records the structure of the eristic move (exclamation treated as
  predication) but does not evaluate whether the move is valid or whether
  puppax has any semantic content that could serve as a predicate. It does not
  claim that Dionysodorus is confused about the nature of exclamations.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_2422
feature_label: athenian_cult_epithets
observation: >-
  Socrates distinguishes the cult titles of the gods among Athenians and
  Ionians: Apollo is called Patrôos (ancestral) because of Ion's birth; Zeus
  is called Herkeios (of the household court) and Phratrios (of the
  brotherhood), not Patrôos; Athena is called Phratria (of the brotherhood).
textual_basis: >-
  At 302c-d Socrates states that "Zeus Patrôos" is not a designation used by
  any Ionian, neither colonists from Athens nor Athenians themselves, and
  provides the correct cult titles: Apollo Patrôos through Ion's lineage,
  Zeus Herkeios and Phratrios, and Athena Phratria.
limits: >-
  This observation only records the textual presence of cult-title
  distinctions. It does not assess the historical accuracy of these claims or
  their broader religious significance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_2423
feature_label: respondent_reluctant_assent
observation: >-
  After being cornered into agreeing that Apollo, Zeus, and Athena are his
  gods, Socrates utters "what am I to suffer / what can I do?" (ti gar
  pathô), marking reluctant assent under eristic pressure.
textual_basis: >-
  At 302d, when Dionysodorus presses him — "didn't you agree they are yours?"
  — Socrates responds "I agreed; for what can I do?" (homologêka, ephên; ti
  gar pathô). The phrase signals that he assents not from conviction but
  because he sees no way to resist the questioning frame.
limits: >-
  This observation identifies a turn-geometry marker of reluctant assent. It
  does not claim that Socrates' underlying beliefs have changed or that the
  argument is sound.
review_status: accepted
```

The mockery is layered: Ctesippus simultaneously deploys the very eristic technique of predicate contradiction that the sophists have been using, turning it back on them."
```yaml
textual_basis: "At 300a Ctesippus tells Euthydemus he seems to be sleeping while not asleep, and that if it is possible for a speaker to say nothing, that is what Euthydemus is doing. limits: "This observation records Ctesippus's mockery and counter-example. It does not claim that the counterexample successfully refutes the sophist's paradox, nor does it attribute any esoteric meaning to the exchange."
```
```yaml
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0201

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: argument_schema
feature_id: feature_candidate_2511
feature_label: tripartite_intermediate_classification
observation: "At 306a-306b, Socrates presents a three-case logical schema for classifying things composed from two elements: (1) from bad and good, the intermediate is better than the bad and worse than the good; (2) from two goods not toward the same end, the intermediate is worse than each with respect to each component's proper end; (3) from two bads not toward the same end, the intermediate is better than each."
textual_basis: "The Greek text at 306a-306b introduces the three cases with parallel markers: the first case is introduced with a men clause concerning things from bad and good; the second with a de clause concerning things from two goods not toward the same end; the third with a de clause concerning things from two bads. Each case specifies how the intermediate compares to its components."
limits: "This observation records the classification structure. It does not assess the logical validity of the schema, its originality, or its role in the broader argument of the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: socratic_criterion_shift
observation: "Socrates redirects Crito's attention from the practitioners of philosophy to philosophy itself as the object of examination. Rather than evaluating philosophy by the character of those who pursue it, he insists that Crito should examine the thing itself (auto to pragma) to determine its worth."
textual_basis: "At 307b Socrates concedes Crito's point that practitioners of philosophy can be ridiculous, but at 307b-307c he pivots: regardless of whether its practitioners are good or bad (eite chrestoi eisin eite poneroi), Crito should test the thing itself (auto to pragma basanisas) and decide on that basis. The criterion shifts from ad hominem assessment of practitioners to direct examination of the subject."
limits: "This observation does not claim that the shift is a formal elenchus with an interlocutor contradiction. It notes only the explicit textual pivot from evaluating people to evaluating the thing itself. The passage does not specify what the test (basanisas) entails."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The dialogue closes with Socrates urging Crito and his children to pursue and practice philosophy with confidence, conditional on Crito's own examination of the thing itself. This is a protreptic exhortation rather than an aporetic or mythic close."
textual_basis: "At 307c Socrates says: if philosophy appears to you as I think it is, then pursue and practice it confidently (tharron dioke kai askei), both yourself and your children (autos te kai ta paidia). The closing speech is a direct exhortation to the philosophical life, framed as a conditional imperative dependent on Crito's own examination."
limits: "This observation does not claim that the protreptic close resolves the dramatic problems raised earlier in the dialogue. It does not assess whether Socrates' confidence about what philosophy is (oion oimai auto ego einai) is justified by the preceding conversation. The protreptic is addressed to Crito alone, not to a general audience."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_2621
feature_label: multiple_crafts_as_parallel_cases
observation: Socrates invokes a list of four pursuits — gymnastics, money-making, rhetoric, and generalship — as parallel cases to establish a general principle that in every pursuit the base practitioners are many and the serious ones are few.
textual_basis: At 307a, Socrates asks Crito whether gymnastics, money-making, rhetoric, and generalship seem noble to him, then uses Crito's assent to these pursuits as parallel cases for the claim that in each the many are base and few are worthy.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates explicitly extends the craft analogy to philosophy within this span, though the immediate context concerns whether Crito should urge a young man toward philosophy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 306e-307a, Crito says that when he looks at any of those claiming to educate people, he is struck (ekpeplegmai) and each appears entirely strange (allokotos) to him upon examination, leaving him at a loss for how to turn the young man to philosophy.
limits: This observation records Crito's reported reaction without claiming that his judgment of the would-be educators is accurate or that Socrates endorses it. The observation does not address whether Crito's perplexity is resolved later in the dialogue.
```

```yaml
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_3170
feature_label: question_chain_escalating_scope
observation: Socrates escalates from a specific performative claim (Dionysodorus can dance) to ever-more-ambitious universal claims: dancing on swords, whirling on a wheel, knowing all things now, always, and from birth. Each question pushes the claim further without a new assent phase.
textual_basis: At 294e Socrates asks whether Dionysodorus can also tumble on swords and whirl on a wheel at his age, given his advanced wisdom. After the reply "nothing he does not" (ouden hoti ou), Socrates presses whether they know everything only now or also always, and whether they knew all things even as newborn children. The brothers answer both together in the affirmative.
limits: This observation notes the escalation structure but does not claim Socrates is refuting the brothers. The assent chain produces no explicit contradiction in this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - "τὰ μὲν ἐπίστασθον τῶν ὄντων, τὰ δὲ οὐκ ἐπίστασθον"
  - "ἥκιστά γε"
  - "οὐδὲν ἄρα ἐπίστασθον"
  - "καὶ μάλα"
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_3175
feature_label: forced_binary_from_qualified_answer
observation: "Socrates asks whether the two brothers know some things and not others (293e). Dionysodorus rejects the qualified framing with 'not at all,' and Socrates then rephrases the question as an all-or-nothing binary ('do you then know nothing?'), to which Dionysodorus answers affirmatively. A qualified epistemic position is collapsed into a forced binary."
textual_basis: "At 293e Socrates asks whether the brothers know some of the things that are and not others, using a men...de construction. Dionysodorus rejects both halves with hekista ge. Socrates then converts the rejection into an all-or-nothing question ('do you then know nothing?'), and Dionysodorus accepts with kai mala. The move from a both/and question to an either/or answer erases the middle ground offered in the initial question."
limits: "This observation does not evaluate whether Dionysodorus is being sincere or whether the sophists' larger position explains their response. It records only the structural collapse of a qualified question into a binary outcome."
review_status: accepted
```
