feature_label: argument_reconstruction_for_confirmation
observation: Socrates restates Euthydemus's eristic argument in step-by-step conditional form and explicitly asks for confirmation at 293d.
textual_basis: After Euthydemus asserts the paradox about being and not-being the same person simultaneously, Socrates says "well then" (see greek_terms), quotes a proverb, then reconstructs the premise chain: if knowing one thing entails knowing everything (because one cannot be both knower and non-knower simultaneously), then since he knows one thing he also knows the target knowledge. He closes by asking whether this is what Euthydemus means (see greek_terms).
limits: This is a reconstruction move, not a refutation. It does not show whether Euthydemus accepts the reconstruction.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: The eristic argument at 293d explicitly invokes the principle of non-contradiction: one cannot be both knowing and not-knowing the same thing at the same time (see greek_terms), and this premise grounds the inference that knowing one thing entails knowing everything.
textual_basis: Socrates' reconstruction of Euthydemus's argument includes the explicit premise that he could not be both knowledgeable and not-knowledgeable at the same time (see greek_terms). This non-contradiction premise is the logical hinge connecting the minor premise (I know one thing) to the conclusion (I know everything, including the target knowledge).
limits: The observation notes the explicit invocation of the non-contradiction principle. It does not assess whether the eristic deployment of this principle is valid, sophistical, or equivocating on the object of knowledge.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: At 296c, the sophist forces Socrates from knowing some things to knowing all things by collapsing the object-scope of knowledge. Socrates notes that the phrase for "what I know" carries no restrictive force once the argument treats it as equivalent to "all things." The sophist then declares this as an admission of omniscience.
textual_basis: The sophist asks whether Socrates could know all things without knowing all things, then treats Socrates' observation that the expression for "what I know" has no limiting force at 296c as agreement that he knows all things at 296c.
limits: This observation identifies an eristic move of collapsing restricted scope into universal scope. It does not assess whether the argument is formally valid or what Plato intends by depicting it.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates reports the brothers' own characterization of their eristic as stitching together mouths ('as you yourselves say'), then immediately subverts it by noting they would seem to stitch up not only others' mouths but also their own, calling this twist 'quite charming' and saying it removes the offensiveness of their arguments."
textual_basis: "Socrates says hoper kai phate (as you yourselves say) attributing the mouth-stitching image to the brothers, then pivots with the observation that they stitch up not only others' mouths but also their own. The praise term charien carries ironic force because the compliment rests on the brothers defeating themselves."
limits: "Does not independently prove Socratic irony is intentional; only shows the text juxtaposes attributed self-description with self-undermining consequence."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Socrates exposes a contradiction between the two brothers: Dionysodorus had just commanded him to refute, but Euthydemus denies that refuting someone who is not lying is possible. Socrates asks how Dionysodorus could command refutation if, as Euthydemus says, no one lies.
textual_basis: At 286e Euthydemus says refuting someone who does not lie is not possible; Socrates immediately asks whether Dionysodorus did not just command refutation, and how one could command the impossible.
limits: The observation records the textual contradiction Socrates identifies; it does not assess whether the brothers' positions are genuinely incompatible or merely equivocal on the meaning of refutation.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 287a Socrates lays out that if no one can err, lie, or be ignorant, then no one errs when acting. He then asks at 287a-287b what the brothers are teachers of, given that they just said they best transmit virtue to willing learners.
limits: The observation records the structure of the reductio Socrates constructs; it does not evaluate whether the brothers' eristic premises actually entail the elimination of all error.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_euthydemus_0252
source_work: Euthydemus
stephanus_span: 301b-301d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/euthydemus.txt
  stephanus_span: 301b-301d
  start_marker: 301b
  end_marker: 301d
  start_char: 63335
  end_char: 64653
  text_sha256: 4e60f9a8a2f34d182fddf36651766284c2fc5f15f7fb0164548ceba7d39a82f1
greek_terms:
  - δημιουργός
  - προσήκει
  - διαλέγεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_4695
feature_label: craft_analogy_strained_to_absurdity
observation: At 301c Socrates praises the brothers as craftsmen who fashion the art of discourse just as other craftsmen fashion what belongs to each craft, using the craftsman framework as a compliment. Dionysodorus then seizes on the notion of what is "proper" to each craft and extends it at 301d to the absurd conclusion that it would be proper to slaughter, skin, boil, and roast the cook himself, and to forge the smith and pot the potter.
textual_basis: Socrates at 301c compares the brothers to craftsmen who produce what belongs to each thing, saying they produce the art of discourse beautifully. Dionysodorus then asks who it is proper to do each craft's tasks, gets Socrates to agree that whoever does what is proper acts correctly, and then at 301d concludes that slaughtering and cooking the cook or forging the smith would count as doing what is proper.
limits: The observation tracks the craft-analogy framework and its reversal; it does not claim what Plato intends by making Socrates initiate the analogy that is then turned against him.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: At 301c-301d Dionysodorus builds an assent chain from self-evident identity claims (the beautiful is beautiful, the same is same, the other is other) through craft-proper-function agreements (it is proper for the smith to forge, the potter to pot, the cook to cut and cook) to the forced absurd conclusion that it is proper to slaughter and cook the cook himself.
textual_basis: Socrates agrees at 301c that the same is same and the other is other, then agrees at 301d that the cook should cut up, skin, boil, and roast, and that whoever does what is proper acts correctly. Dionysodorus then concludes that slaughtering and cooking the cook, or forging the smith, would be doing what is proper. Socrates explicitly notes he has agreed but asks for pardon.
limits: The observation identifies the assent-chain structure and its absurd terminus; it does not classify whether this counts as a genuine elenchus or a sophistical parody of one.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: >-

The narrative voice is Crito's, not the primary dialogue narrator, and the exchange with the unnamed man is indirect.

  At 304e Socrates calls philosophy "a charming thing," and at 305a the critic retorts that it is "worthy of nothing," that the practitioners are "worthless and laughable," and that Socrates' companion was "absurd" for presenting himself to people who care nothing for what they say.
